## Semantic Chunks
### Section: Introduction
- **Level**: `intro`
- **Hash**: `fb68c7777b016f59039e963471cab219463956f4dbbc6d90bbc3a8adc1b5ca4b`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-can-i-tell-the-difference-between-ai-writing-and-a-student-improving` (Similarity: 0.9081)

```text
A student submits a polished essay. The argument is sophisticated, the vocabulary is advanced, and the paper appears much stronger than their previous work. Then you ask a simple question: "Can you explain what you mean in this paragraph?" The student struggles to answer. What should you do?

A student's inability to explain their own essay can be a meaningful warning sign, but it should not automatically be treated as proof that the student used ChatGPT, another AI tool, or someone else's work .

Students may be nervous, forget details, or understand an idea in writing better than verbally. At the same time, repeated difficulty explaining central arguments, sources, vocabulary, or the writing process can justify a closer review.

Checkmark Plagiarism can support that review with AI detection , essay writing playback , static AI detection, plagiarism detection , autograding , and integrations with Canvas and Google Classroom .
```

### Section: Don't Treat One Bad Answer as Proof
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `814aac5302bf71ad4da0cb5c031daaab90d6cc4ad72e39080126eecd0ae92d67`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-ask-a-student-who-says-an-ai-detector-is-wrong` (Similarity: 0.8289)

```text
Students often struggle when put on the spot: nervousness, public speaking anxiety, or completing an assignment several days earlier can cause hesitation. Look for an overall pattern of comprehension across the entire paper rather than treating a single stumbling response as a confession.
```

### Section: Start With the Main Argument
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `b3f373ecb371805e0778fefd6d6cd15229c55ff15c8bd0a3529225a14ecd7039`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.9200)

```text
Begin broadly: "What is the main argument of your essay?" The student does not need to recite their thesis verbatim. Explaining the core idea in simple, conversational language is a strong sign of genuine conceptual ownership.
```

### Section: Ask Them to Explain a Specific Paragraph
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `e64ffe79d181bc3a352029c121462faa3a914a2e27b72ec45d9339d4ad0ec7ad`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-look-for-when-a-students-writing-suddenly-improves` (Similarity: 0.8857)

```text
Choose a central paragraph and ask: "What are you saying in this paragraph, and how does it support your thesis?" A student who authored the paper should recognize the reasoning behind major arguments.
```

### Section: Ask About Advanced Vocabulary
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `ae0199cf43b650b81c5d0b45941584f08bfe8003807e26fc21c9f29af2a505bb`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.9309)

```text
If the paper uses specialized terms such as "institutional inequality," "cognitive dissonance," or "socioeconomic stratification," ask: "What does that phrase mean in the context of your argument?" You are looking for understanding in context, not a dictionary definition.
```

### Section: Ask Why They Chose Particular Evidence
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `7ac3b27f51f7374d689c29c4065c7fb1efa9518f5a6ae4cb1d67b09974e2969e`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8803)

```text
Ask: "Why did you choose this quotation? How did this source shape your thinking?" Students who conducted authentic research can usually explain why certain evidence was selected.
```

### Section: Verify the Sources
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `d128b4a99be98209370940d87fa2ac46d73a2e80b29e6afe4eaf0ea9b5dafdee`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-can-i-tell-if-a-student-actually-wrote-their-essay` (Similarity: 0.9226)

```text
If a student struggles to identify where a citation came from, check whether the source, author, and quotes actually exist in databases. Generative AI tools frequently invent fake references that look persuasive on the page.
```

### Section: Ask About the Writing Process
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `7238b26aaa976c21d9cc2787acdd6033fbbb970c75a8a1a7163f728f16e23ccb`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.9578)

```text
Ask: "Can you walk me through how you wrote this assignment from start to finish?" Inquire about outlining, rough drafting, hard sections, revisions, tools used, and tutoring support.
```

### Section: Ask What They Revised
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `f00881639956727cb7008e5d1b545f000c9ff617a19163b79f6f0ac32df8ff07`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-questions-should-i-ask-a-student-about-a-suspicious-assignment` (Similarity: 0.8410)

```text
Ask: "Which paragraph changed the most while you were drafting?" Authors almost always remember sections they struggled to write, shortened, or rewrote.
```

### Section: Look at How the Essay Was Created
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `72814a012aed3491773500bd4f7f2d3b52e7b6aa29acfd9919c5e55f8c5ad30f`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/can-teachers-detect-ai-without-using-an-ai-detector` (Similarity: 0.8352)

```text
Situation A: Genuine Drafting

Student hesitates on one paragraph 
 Playback shows gradual multi-day drafting 
 Student has notes, outlines, & rough drafts 
 Fluently explains the rest of the essay

Situation B: Multiple Concerns

Student cannot explain major arguments 
 Entire essay appeared suddenly in document 
 Multiple citations do not exist in databases 
 Writing style diverges completely from past work
```


### Section: Avoid Asking the Student to Reproduce the Essay From Memory
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `f3ececa3bd0eb2f272066018bc71488745cfa11e64c8d34f0688432ee65ade18`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.8176)

```text
Do not demand that a student reproduce paragraphs word-for-word from memory under pressure. Focus on core conceptual familiarity, arguments, and drafting choices.
```

### Section: Do Not Say "This Is Too Good for You"
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `2cd37499ec503f65aa8b3e0ca93be108c23ac6b867c90107faa40925b292c291`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.8984)

```text
Avoid attacking student ability. Instead, focus on specific, observable differences: "This assignment differs substantially from your previous writing, and I want to understand what changed in your process."
```

### Section: A Practical Process When a Student Can't Explain Their Essay
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `182e40dd0f47961d9a34ebb273c3e19c5283874ec8ed465e73c62e1d912bc52e`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/can-i-prove-that-a-student-used-chatgpt` (Similarity: 0.8823)

```text
Practical 14-Step Sequence When a Student Can't Explain Their Essay:

1. Do not treat one hesitant or difficult answer as automatic proof of misconduct. 
 2. Ask the student to summarize the central thesis in their own conversational words. 
 3. Discuss one or two important paragraphs rather than obscure isolated sentences. 
 4. Ask them to explain key terminology and vocabulary in the context of their argument. 
 5. Ask why they chose specific sources, quotations, or illustrative examples. 
 6. Ask them to describe their writing and drafting process from beginning to end. 
 7. Compare the essay against multiple previous writing baselines. 
 8. Verify suspicious sources, author names, and direct quotes in academic databases. 
 9. Review essay writing playback when available to check drafting progression. 
 10. Use AI detection as one additional signal rather than a definitive verdict. 
 11. Inquire neutrally about AI assistance, writing tools, or tutoring support. 
 12. Give the student an opportunity to provide earlier drafts, notes, or version history. 
 13. Evaluate whether all gathered evidence tells a consistent and plausible story. 
 14. Apply established school academic-integrity policies if significant concerns remain.
```


### Section: An Inability to Explain the Essay Is a Question, Not the Answer

- **Hash**: `56344c032c0dd30135640da92c8faf66e313cfee7287f5d87142c43f6e71431c`

```text
Checkmark Plagiarism supports this evidence-based approach with AI detection, essay writing playback, static AI detection, plagiarism detection, autograding, and integrations with Canvas and Google Classroom.

See how Checkmark pairs essay writing playback with multi-signal detection to resolve authorship questions fairly. View a sample report or request a demonstration .
```
